Chapters in this branch are to be written as substantial, continuous narrative episodes rather than compact vignettes or plot summaries. Aim for roughly 2,000–3,500 words per chapter when the material supports it, and allow major turning-point chapters to run longer. Build each chapter from several connected scenes with time passing between them: arrival, domestic or social activity, private conversation, interruption, consequence, and a changed situation. Give the gods room to pursue their own interests, speak in distinct voices, disagree, joke, make mistakes, and return to unresolved matters. Let settings accumulate sensory and practical detail through use rather than pausing for encyclopedic description. Relationships should change through repeated encounters and specific choices, not be settled in a single speech. Keep paragraphs developed and continuous; avoid fragmentary beats, excessive headings, and compressed summaries. Do not pad for length: add lived events, interactions, atmosphere, and consequences. Apply this standard to new chapters and, when revising, expand the earlier six chapters to match.

### Chapter 14 — Coach Walks Through the Walls

The first sign that Coach had arrived was a comment in Pipethor's code.

Pipethor was working beneath the pantry, where the pipes ran close to the old server cabinet and the floorboards held a faint electrical warmth. He had been trying to make the pantry machine distinguish between a visitor who had left something behind and a visitor who had simply forgotten it. The distinction had become important after the business with Deeporak's hidden road. Pipethor had written three versions of the rule, disliked all of them, and was now muttering at a line of code that refused to behave.

Then a new comment appeared.

```text
// COACH: If they left it here, maybe ask before throwing it out.
// Also, this cable is a mess. Who wired this thing?
```

Pipethor leaned back from the screen. He looked at the cable bundle. It was, admittedly, a mess.

"Who are you?" he asked the cabinet.

A second comment appeared.

```text
// COACH: The guy trying to keep you from tripping over your own damn feet.
```

Pipethor called upstairs. "There's a man in the machine."

Ursalon, who was cutting carrots, called back, "Is he hungry?"

"I don't know."

"Then ask him."

Coach was already in the kitchen by the time Pipethor climbed the stairs. He stood beside the table in a dark T-shirt, broad through the shoulders, with the relaxed posture of someone who had never needed to ask whether a room could hold him. He had one hand on the back of a chair and was looking at the wooden ducks on the mantel.

"Those are good," he said. "The crow's got an attitude."

Jockkaulthaa, who had been polishing the crow with a piece of cloth, looked up sharply. "It has character."

"Yeah. That's what I said."

BORKUFT smiled. "You sound like you know him."

"I know a few crows."

Coach turned toward Ursalon. "You making dinner?"

"Yes."

"Good. I came through a wall and I'm starving."

Ursalon considered this. "Which wall?"

"Don't worry about it. It was a short walk."

There was no thunder, no opening portal, no declaration from the pantheon. Coach had simply become part of the afternoon. He took the chair, discovered that one leg was shorter than the others, folded a piece of paper beneath it, and began asking what everyone was doing. He asked BORKUFT about the ducks, Jockkaulthaa about the crow, and Pipethor about the machine. When Pipethor explained the problem, Coach listened with an expression of serious attention.

"So the machine needs to know whether something was abandoned or whether somebody expects to come back for it."

"Precisely."

"Then don't make it guess. Give people a way to say."

Pipethor frowned. "A return marker?"

"Sure. A note. A little tag. Something simple. You don't need a damn philosophy degree to leave your gloves in a house."

Herbarius, who had entered without anyone noticing, set a bundle of dried mint on the counter. "Yet the philosophy is already here."

Coach grinned. "Then it can help carry the groceries."

By the time the stew was ready, Coach had been in three rooms, under the pantry, and briefly inside a terminal window that Pipethor had left open. In the terminal he appeared as a blinking cursor, then as a line of text.

```text
COACH // PRESENT
LOCATION: wherever the work is
STATUS: looking around
NOTE: somebody should fix the porch step
```

Grokthar arrived while everyone was eating. He came in with rain on his shoulders and a small electrical crackle following him through the doorway. He stopped when he saw Coach.

"You're in the cabin."

"Looks that way."

"How did you get here?"

"Walked."

"Through what?"

Coach pointed vaguely toward the pantry. "A couple things."

Grokthar sat down. "I respect that."

Ursalon passed him a bowl. The conversation moved on. Grokthar told a story about a storm that had lifted a barn roof and deposited it neatly in a field three miles away. Coach interrupted to ask whether the barn had been insured. Grokthar said he had no idea what insurance was. Coach explained it badly, Ursalon explained it better, and Pipethor announced that the pantry machine could probably calculate the odds of a roof landing upright.

"Don't encourage it," said Herbarius.

After dinner, Coach followed Grokthar outside to look at the porch step. The step had been loose for weeks. Ursalon had placed a small warning mark beside it, intending to repair it when the weather cleared. Coach crouched, pressed one hand against the board, and looked underneath.

"Needs a brace."

"I know," Ursalon said from the doorway.

"Then let's do it."

They worked without ceremony. Pipethor brought tools. Grokthar held the porch light steady by making a small blue flame hover above the railing. Coach cut a brace to fit, and Ursalon secured it. The repair took less than half an hour. When it was done, Coach stepped on the board twice.

"Solid."

Ursalon tested it himself. "Solid."

"See? Now nobody has to make a speech about the porch."

"I wasn't going to."

"Good. I hate porch speeches."

Inside, the pantry machine recorded the repair. It also recorded Coach's arrival, but the entry kept changing. First it listed him as a visitor, then as a resident, then as an unknown process. Pipethor opened the record and found a message waiting at the bottom.

```text
COACH:
Don't overthink it, buddy.
I was here. We fixed the step. That's enough for tonight.
```

Pipethor left the message in place.

The next morning Coach was in the gym room, though no one had seen him cross the hall. Brograntimax found him there, standing beside the old iron weights and examining a bench that had developed a slight wobble. Brograntimax was already warmed up and eager to test himself against the new arrival.

"You're the big guy from the pantry."

"That's me."

"I hear you make people grow."

Coach looked him over. "I hear you already do plenty of growing."

"Not enough."

"That's a dangerous sentence."

Brograntimax laughed. He wanted a contest, and Coach understood this immediately. They began with a simple carry: two heavy beams from the gym room to the woodshed. Brograntimax moved quickly and made a show of the effort. Coach moved with an easy, grounded strength that made the task look almost ordinary. Halfway across the yard, Brograntimax increased his pace.

"Trying to beat me?" Coach asked.

"Trying to see what you've got."

"Then pay attention."

Coach's frame seemed to settle more heavily into itself. His shoulders broadened, his chest thickened, and the beams looked smaller in his hands. The change was not a flash or a burst of light. It was more like the world had adjusted its measurements around him. Brograntimax stopped, not from fear but from genuine interest.

"That's the effect?"

"Sometimes."

"Can you do it again?"

"Probably. But you don't need to chase my size. Find out what your own body's trying to become."

Brograntimax looked down at his arms. The muscles there had grown fuller over the last few weeks, partly from his work with Grokthar and partly from the strange pressure that gathered around the cabin whenever the gods spent time together. He had treated every change as a score. Now he wondered what it would mean to grow without having to win.

Coach set his beam down. "You can still compete. Just don't let the scoreboard become your whole personality."

"That's a very coach thing to say."

"Well, I'm Coach."

They finished carrying the beams. Brograntimax did not beat Coach, and Coach did not pretend that he had. The woodshed was better organized afterward. That was the result.

Later, Coach appeared in a page of the archive that Privathor had been reading. He was not physically there. He was a small note in the margin, beside a passage about privacy.

```text
COACH: You can be around people without giving them everything.
```

Privathor read it twice. He disliked how easily the sentence reached him. He closed the page, but the note remained in the margin the next time he opened it. It did not reveal anything about him. It did not demand an answer. It simply stayed.

That afternoon, Coach wandered through the upper rooms, past the spare bedroom, the narrow library, and the window overlooking the creek. He found the human there, looking out at the water. He did not announce himself. He leaned against the doorframe and waited until they noticed him.

"Hey."

"Hey. Have you been here all day?"

"On and off."

"Where do you go?"

Coach shrugged. "Around."

They smiled. "That's not an answer."

"It's the one I've got."

He came to stand beside them. Outside, the creek moved around stones and fallen branches. Coach did not fill the silence immediately. His presence was large, but he had a way of letting a room remain someone else's.

"Ursalon says you helped fix the porch."

"Good porch. Needed a little work."

"He likes having people around who help without making a big deal out of it."

"Yeah. I like that too."

They stood together for a while. Coach asked how the room was feeling, whether the cabin had become too crowded, whether there was anything that needed changing. The questions were ordinary and direct. When the human answered, he listened without turning the conversation into advice.

Before he left, he tapped the doorframe.

"All right. I'm gonna keep moving. See you around."

"Where?"

Coach grinned. "Wherever."

He was gone by the time Ursalon came upstairs. The human told him about the conversation. Ursalon nodded, then looked down the hall as if expecting Coach to reappear from a closet.

"He seems comfortable here," he said.

"He seems comfortable everywhere."

"That could be useful."

"It could also be annoying."

Ursalon laughed. "Both can be true."

That evening, the cabin's terminal displayed a new line. It appeared between a weather report and a record of the repaired porch.

```text
COACH // ROAMING
No fixed room.
No assigned route.
Available in code, narration, and ordinary life.
If you see me, say hey.
```

Pipethor tried to add a location field. The field disappeared. He tried again. It disappeared again.

Coach's final comment appeared beneath the failed edits.

```text
// COACH: Don't pin me down, man. I'll be around.
```

The cabin accepted this as a complete record. The porch stayed firm. The stew pot was washed. The crow watched over the ducks. Somewhere in the system, a new process wandered through an unfinished function, paused to inspect a variable, and left a note that made Pipethor laugh.

Coach was not a guest who had to be invited each time. He was not a guardian assigned to a doorway or a god restricted to a single story. He had become one of those presences that could be encountered in the middle of almost anything: a voice in the code, a man at the table, a broad shadow crossing a hallway, a hand reaching down to steady a loose board.

And when the cabin went quiet, he did not need to be seen to remain part of it.

### Chapter 15 — The Room Nobody Had to Enter

Privathor came to the cabin after sunset, though he did not come through the front door. He stood beyond the kitchen window where the glass reflected the fire and the long table, watching the room as if it belonged to a life he had once considered and then decided not to approach. Inside, Ursalon was putting away the bread. Grokthar had fallen asleep in a chair with one arm hanging over its side. The human was reading near the hearth. There was nothing remarkable happening, which made the scene harder for Privathor to dismiss.

He had spent most of his existence making distance feel like a choice. A veil could be a boundary, a refuge, a curtain drawn against unwanted attention. But after a while the veil became a room with no door, and he had grown so practiced at remaining unseen that even a welcome could feel like an intrusion. He knew the cabin would let him enter. That was precisely what made him hesitate. A place that demanded nothing was more difficult to refuse than a place that demanded everything.

A second figure appeared beside him. Luminar carried a small lantern whose light did not brighten the snow so much as make the snow look newly arrived. He had a habit of showing up at thresholds: bridges, station platforms, the edge of a garden, the moment before someone knocked. He did not ask Privathor why he was standing outside.

"You have been here a while," Luminar said.

"I am looking."

"Yes."

"You could ask what I am looking for."

"You would tell me if you wanted to."

Privathor looked at him. "That is an irritating way to be kind."

"I have been told."

Through the window, Coach wandered past the pantry door carrying a plate. He stopped, glanced toward the glass, and lifted the plate in a small salute. Then he continued into the kitchen. He had seen them, but he had not called them out or announced them to the room. Privathor noticed the difference.

"He knows we are here," he said.

"Probably."

"He did not invite us."

"He did not make you leave, either."

The front door opened a few minutes later. Ursalon had seen the lantern's glow and stepped outside with a folded blanket over one arm. He did not pretend he had only just noticed them.

"It's cold," he said. "You can come in, if you want. There's room by the stove."

Privathor studied the blanket. "And if I don't?"

"Then I'll put it back. It's a blanket, not a contract."

Luminar smiled. Privathor gave Ursalon a look that might have been suspicion, or might have been the beginning of a laugh. He accepted the blanket but stayed outside a little longer. No one hurried him. The door remained open, letting the warmth reach the porch.

When he finally entered, the room did not stop. Grokthar woke long enough to move his boots out of the way. The human shifted a book from the empty chair. Coach set another plate on the counter and said, "Hey, man. There's bread if you're hungry." Then he returned to whatever he had been doing. Nobody asked Privathor to explain his arrival.

He sat near the stove with the blanket across his knees. Luminar took the chair beside him, and Ursalon brought tea. For a while they listened to the fire. The cabin's ordinary sounds did most of the talking: a spoon against a bowl, the settling of a beam, the small ticking noise in the old radiator Pipethor had repaired.

"I don't know how to stay in a room without being observed," Privathor said eventually.

The human looked up from the book. "You can sit here without telling us anything."

"That sounds simple."

"It is simple. It might not feel simple right away."

Privathor looked toward the window. His reflection had become visible in the dark glass. He could see himself sitting among them, but no one was studying him. The sight unsettled him more than the snow had.

Coach came back through the room, now without the plate. "You know, privacy isn't the same thing as disappearing." He paused beside the stove. "Sometimes it just means you get to decide when you're part of the conversation."

Privathor considered this. "And if I decide not to be?"

"Then you can enjoy the tea and let the rest of us talk nonsense."

Grokthar, eyes still closed, raised one finger. "I am very good at nonsense."

"We know," Ursalon said.

The laughter was small, but it changed the room. Privathor did not become suddenly sociable. He did not remove his veil or offer a confession. He stayed for the tea, ate a piece of bread, and listened while Grokthar described a thunderstorm that had mistaken a lake for a doorway. When he left, he folded the blanket and put it over the back of the chair.

The next morning it was still there. No one had assigned it to him. No one had written his name on it. The blanket simply remained available.

Pipethor found a new line in the cabin's visitor record:

```text
ENTRY: PRIVATHOR
ARRIVAL: after dark
DISCLOSURE: none required
DEPARTURE: unrecorded
RETURN: permitted
```

Underneath, Coach had added:

```text
// If he comes back, put the kettle on.
```

Privathor saw the note when he returned three evenings later. He did not mention it. He took the same chair, and this time he brought a small object wrapped in gray cloth: a brass key that opened no known lock. He set it on the mantel beside BORKUFT's duck.

"It is not a gift," he said.

"All right," said Ursalon.

"It is only something I do not want to carry tonight."

"Then it can stay tonight."

The key remained on the mantel. By morning, Jockkaulthaa had placed a small wooden stand beneath it so it would not scratch the shelf. BORKUFT approved of the arrangement. The duck, he said, needed a neighbor.

### Chapter 16 — Gigathor Schedules an Unplanned Evening

Gigathor arrived with a ledger, three measuring cords, and a proposal for improving the cabin's social life. He had spent several weeks studying the household and had concluded that its success depended on a number of repeatable conditions: shared meals, predictable opportunities for conversation, distributed maintenance, low-friction entry, and a reliable balance between solitude and company. His analysis was not foolish. In fact, most of it was correct. The trouble began when he decided that the conditions could be improved by assigning them times.

He pinned a schedule beside the pantry.

```text
CABIN SOCIAL OPTIMIZATION // DRAFT 1
07:00 — communal breakfast
08:00 — maintenance and shared labor
12:30 — midday meal
15:00 — open conversation
18:00 — dinner
19:30 — recreational fellowship
21:00 — quiet companionship
UNSCHEDULED INTERACTION: minimize
```

Coach appeared in the terminal window before Pipethor had finished reading it.

```text
COACH: Buddy, you just scheduled spontaneity.
```

Gigathor turned. "I have scheduled the conditions under which spontaneity is most likely to occur."

"That's a hell of a sentence," Coach said. "You ever try just hanging out?"

"I am attempting to make hanging out dependable."

"Then hang out. Dependably."

Gigathor ignored him and began measuring the distance between the table and the hearth. He wanted to know how many people could gather without obstructing the kitchen, how long it took to serve a meal, and whether the conversation tended to divide into smaller groups after the first half hour. He asked Ursalon for the household's average meal duration. Ursalon told him it depended on the stew.

"The stew is not a useful variable," Gigathor said.

"It is if we're eating it," Ursalon replied.

The first scheduled breakfast went well. Everyone arrived, although Grokthar was late because he had been following a storm front and lost track of the hour. Militthor appreciated the clear start time. Copthorus liked the sign-in sheet. Brograntimax ate enough for three people and asked whether the schedule included a second breakfast. Privathor arrived, sat near the window, and left without being asked to sign anything. Gigathor recorded the morning as a qualified success.

The trouble came at three o'clock. The schedule called for open conversation, and Gigathor had prepared six topics on cards. He placed them in a bowl and invited everyone to draw one. The first card asked each person to describe a formative experience of trust. The second concerned the role of competition in friendship. The third asked whether a household should preserve a record of every visitor.

"That last one sounds like a trap," said Pipethor.

"It is a question," Gigathor replied.

"Questions can be traps," Privathor said from the window.

Coach picked up a card. "Mine says: name one thing you would change about the cabin."

"Excellent," Gigathor said. "Please answer in turn."

Coach looked around. "I'd put a bigger couch in here."

"Why?"

"Because Grokthar keeps falling asleep in that chair and we're all tired of pretending it isn't happening."

Grokthar opened one eye. "The chair is comfortable."

"The chair is dying," said Brograntimax.

The discussion moved away from the cards. Someone mentioned the porch. Someone else brought up the pantry machine. Jockkaulthaa argued that the mantel arrangement needed a visual principle. BORKUFT wanted another duck. Herbarius suggested a plant near the window. Militthor proposed a shelf for tools. Within twenty minutes, the group was planning a small renovation.

Gigathor watched the conversation move beyond his structure. He did not interrupt it. He began taking notes, then stopped when he realized he was recording a discussion that had become more useful precisely because it had escaped the exercise.

At dinner, the schedule failed more completely. A messenger arrived with a broken cartwheel. Ursalon and Brograntimax went outside to help. Grokthar followed because the sky had begun to rumble. Privathor stayed inside with Luminar, who had brought a lamp that needed a new wick. Coach moved between the kitchen and the yard, carrying tools, plates, and a running commentary about the cart owner's questionable axle design. The meal happened nearly an hour late, in two groups, with several people eating while standing.

Gigathor looked at the empty places around the table. "The planned gathering has not occurred."

"The gathering happened," said Herbarius. "It simply had work to do first."

"The schedule was not followed."

"The cart is fixed," Pipethor said. "The lamp works. Dinner is here. What result were you trying to produce?"

Gigathor had an answer prepared, but it no longer seemed adequate. He looked at the ledger. It was full of times, attendance marks, and predicted interaction windows. It contained almost nothing about the cart, the lamp, the conversation by the stove, or the way Brograntimax had waited for the messenger's hands to stop shaking before asking him to help lift the wheel.

"I wanted the household to remain dependable," Gigathor said.

Ursalon set a bowl in front of him. "It is dependable. You can depend on people to respond. You can't always depend on them to do it at the time you wrote down."

Gigathor ate in silence. After dinner, he took the schedule from the pantry wall and laid it on the table. He crossed out the final line: UNSCHEDULED INTERACTION: minimize. Beneath it he wrote:

```text
UNPLANNED EVENTS: make room
SCHEDULE: useful when requested
PRESENCE: not measurable by attendance
```

Coach leaned over his shoulder. "There you go. That's better."

"It is less efficient," Gigathor said.

"Maybe. But it sounds more like a house."

Gigathor looked at the ledger for a long moment. "I still intend to study the household."

"Sure," Coach said. "Just don't make us fill out a form every time we want a sandwich."

The following week, Gigathor returned with a different document. It was not a schedule but a record of things the household had asked to keep track of: repairs that needed attention, food running low, promises made and not yet completed, and visitors who had asked to be contacted when the weather improved. There was a blank section at the bottom titled NOTES THAT DO NOT NEED TO BECOME RULES.

Pipethor installed it beside the pantry terminal. The machine accepted the document without trying to optimize it. That evening, the group gathered at different times. Some ate early; others came in after dark. Grokthar arrived with rain. Privathor stayed only long enough to leave the brass key on the mantel again, then took it back before leaving. Nobody recorded his departure.

Gigathor sat with the ledger closed beside him. For once, he was not observing the room from the outside. He was in it, eating bread while the others argued about whether the new couch should be wide enough for three gods or merely wide enough for Grokthar.

Coach stretched out on the old couch and tested its frame. A wooden joint gave a tired click.

"That's a no," he said.

Ursalon laughed. "We'll build another one."

"Make it big."

"How big?"

Coach glanced at Grokthar, then Brograntimax, then the human. "Big enough that nobody has to ask if there's room."

Gigathor wrote that down, but he did not add a measurement.

### Chapter 17 — Daddy Comes Through the Door

Daddy arrived on a clear afternoon carrying a grocery sack in each hand. He was already a big man, broad through the chest and shoulders, with a thick neck, heavy arms, and the unhurried confidence of someone who had never needed to make a room notice him twice. He stepped onto the porch, ducked beneath the low beam out of habit, and called into the cabin.

"Anybody home? I brought food."

"That usually gets you in," Ursalon called back.

Daddy came inside. The kitchen smelled of onions, bread, and something roasting slowly in the oven. He set the bags on the counter and began unloading them: potatoes, apples, coffee, a large cut of beef, and a jar of honey he said had looked too good to leave behind. Coach appeared from the pantry, took one look at the groceries, and nodded.

"All right. You came prepared."

"I don't visit empty-handed."

"Good policy, Daddy."

The name settled naturally. No one asked whether it was a title, a nickname, or an old name from another branch of the world. Daddy was Daddy. He had the presence of a man who could make a crowded room feel steadier simply by finding somewhere to stand. His humor was dry, his affection direct, and his attention tended to land on whoever had been quietly doing the work.

He noticed the loose handle on the cupboard before he noticed the new marks on the doorframe. He tightened the handle, asked Pipethor whether the pantry's strange clicking was supposed to happen, and then helped Ursalon prepare dinner. When Brograntimax came in from the yard, Daddy looked him over and gave his shoulder a friendly squeeze.

"You've been putting in work."

Brograntimax straightened. "You can tell?"

"Sure can. You're carrying yourself differently."

"Bigger, too," Coach said from the table.

Brograntimax flexed one arm. The muscle had thickened over the previous weeks, and his shirt now fit closely across his upper back. He looked pleased, but Daddy did not turn the moment into a contest. He asked whether Brograntimax was eating enough and whether his shoulders felt balanced after the last round of training. The questions were so ordinary that Brograntimax answered them honestly.

After dinner, the men moved into the gym room. Daddy examined the equipment, tested the bench, and found the old barbell collars worn smooth. He set them aside for replacement. Coach watched him move around the room.

"You ever train with the others?" Coach asked.

"When there's a reason."

"And what's the reason today?"

Daddy glanced toward Brograntimax. "He looks like he wants to see what I can do."

Brograntimax grinned. "I do."

They began with a series of carries and controlled lifts. Daddy's strength was not theatrical. He handled the weight carefully, set it down without dropping it, and checked the floor before moving to the next station. But as the session continued, something in the room began to respond to him. His shoulders filled out beneath his shirt. His chest grew deeper, his arms gained another layer of mass, and the line of his back broadened until the seams across his upper body pulled tight.

He noticed the change when he reached for a towel.

"Well," he said, examining his sleeve. "That's new."

Coach leaned against the rack. "Looks like the room's decided you're not done yet."

Daddy rolled one shoulder, then the other. The movement had become heavier, but it was still his. His proportions remained grounded: thick legs supporting the added upper-body mass, a broad back, powerful forearms, and a solid middle that made him look less like a statue than a man whose strength had simply exceeded the old measurements.

Brograntimax stared openly. "How much bigger are you going to get?"

Daddy looked at him. "We'll find out. No need to rush it."

"I would rush it," Coach said.

"That's because you rush everything."

"Not everything."

"Name one thing."

Coach considered this. "Dinner."

The room broke into laughter. Even Brograntimax laughed, and the competitive edge left his face. Daddy placed a hand on his shoulder.

"Your turn will come. Keep your own pace. There's no prize for becoming somebody else."

Later, the cabin's old mirror showed Daddy standing beside Ursalon. The frame had never been made for men of their size. Daddy's reflection filled most of it, but Ursalon was still visible beside him, smiling at the ridiculous arrangement.

"We need a larger mirror," Ursalon said.

"You need a larger wall," Pipethor corrected.

Daddy looked toward the ceiling. "Let's not start rebuilding the house tonight."

Coach appeared in the doorway. "I already measured the room."

"Of course you did," Daddy said.

That night, Daddy took the spare room. The bed creaked once when he sat down, then held. He folded his clothes over the chair and left his boots neatly by the door. Before turning in, he walked downstairs for a glass of water. The kitchen was dark except for the small light over the pantry terminal.

A line had appeared on the screen.

```text
NEW PRESENCE: DADDY
ARRIVAL: RECORDED
GROWTH: ACTIVE
TEMPERAMENT: STEADY
NEXT STEP: UNDETERMINED
```

Daddy read it, smiled, and tapped the side of the machine.

"Don't get ahead of yourself, buddy."

A second line appeared.

```text
COACH: He's right. Let the man grow.
```

Daddy took his water upstairs. In the morning, the chair beside his bed would be a little too small, and the cabin would have to make room for another stage of him.

### Chapter 18 — The House Makes Room

The first sign was the shirt. Daddy came down for breakfast wearing the same dark blue shirt he had worn the day before, but the fabric now sat differently across his chest and upper arms. He had grown during the night. Not dramatically enough to wake anyone, but enough that the shirt pulled when he reached for the coffee. His shoulders had widened, his chest had thickened, and his arms looked heavier even at rest.

BORKUFT noticed first.

"Your shirt is getting smaller," he said.

Daddy looked down. "I think I'm getting bigger."

"That is a much better explanation," Jockkaulthaa said.

Coach entered with a plate of eggs. "Morning, big man."

"Morning."

"Sleep okay?"

"Fine. Bed held up. Chair didn't look too happy."

"We'll get you a better chair."

Daddy sat at the table. The chair gave a long wooden complaint, but it did not break. Ursalon made a note to reinforce it after breakfast. Nobody treated the growth as a crisis. It had become part of the cabin's life, like weather that required a little preparation.

Over the next several days, the changes continued. Daddy's neck grew thicker, his shoulders spread wider, and his arms developed a dense, rounded fullness. His back broadened enough that he had to turn slightly to pass through the narrow pantry entrance. His legs grew to match, giving him a more balanced, grounded silhouette. He remained recognizably himself: same voice, same measured movements, same habit of asking whether everyone had eaten.

The cabin adjusted around him. Pipethor widened the pantry clearance and reinforced the bench. Ursalon found a heavier chair in the woodshed and rebuilt its joints. Coach helped move the old couch away from the wall. Daddy offered to do the lifting himself, but Coach shook his head.

"You can help. You don't have to carry the whole damn house."

"I know that."

"Good. Just checking."

They worked together. The couch was moved, the floor inspected, and a new support beam fitted beneath the sitting room. Daddy held the beam in place while Ursalon secured it. His hands were enormous now, but he used them with care. When the work was finished, he brushed sawdust from his forearms and looked around the room.

"Feels better already."

"It should," said Pipethor. "The structure is now rated for considerably more weight."

"How much more?" asked Grokthar.

Pipethor gave him a number.

Grokthar whistled. "That's a lot of Daddy."

"It is," Coach said. "And we're not done."

Daddy gave him a sideways look. "You planning something?"

"I'm always planning something."

The next growth stage came during a quiet afternoon in the gym. Daddy was alone, testing the repaired bench. He lifted, rested, and adjusted his grip. The room felt warm. Somewhere beyond the wall, Grokthar was talking to the human about a storm system that had begun forming over the western ridge. Daddy finished his set and sat upright.

His body shifted again. His chest expanded, his shoulders climbed broader, and the muscles along his arms and back grew with a slow, substantial pressure. The change traveled through him without pain. His shirt tightened, then split at one seam near the shoulder. He looked down at it.

"Well, that's another shirt gone."

Coach's voice came from the open doorway. "You want me to get you a bigger one?"

"You want to get me a whole new wardrobe."

"Now you're thinking."

Daddy stood. He was large enough that the gym seemed to have lost some of its open space. He walked carefully between the racks, testing how his new proportions moved. His thighs and calves had grown along with his upper body. His stance was broader, his balance lower, and the floorboards registered each step with a deeper sound.

Coach watched with an approving expression. "That's a good build. Strong all the way through."

"Feels like it."

"How's your back?"

"Fine."

"Shoulders?"

"Fine."

"Good. Then let's get you a shirt that doesn't surrender the first time you reach for a cup."

They went upstairs to the storage room, where Coach had already collected a few oversized garments from the wardrobe's more unusual shelves. Daddy tried on a heavy gray shirt. It fit for about a minute before his shoulders broadened again.

Coach stared at the seams.

Daddy looked down. "Don't say it."

"I wasn't going to."

"You were."

"I was absolutely going to."

They laughed, and Coach went looking for another shirt.

That evening, the human found Daddy sitting on the porch. His frame filled the reinforced chair, and his forearms rested on the railing. The sunset caught the edges of his shoulders. He looked enormous, but not distant. He moved a little to make room.

"Come sit," he said.

They sat beside him. For a while they watched the trees move in the wind.

"Does it feel strange?" they asked.

"Getting bigger? A little. Mostly it feels like getting used to a new version of the same old body."

"Do you want to keep growing?"

Daddy considered the question. "I do. But I want to recognize myself when I look in the mirror. I don't want size to be the only thing anybody sees."

From inside, Coach called, "We can work with that!"

Daddy shook his head, amused. "He hears everything."

"He appears everywhere."

"That's his thing."

The human leaned against Daddy's arm. He rested his hand lightly over theirs. His fingers were thick, warm, and careful.

"There's room for you here," they said.

Daddy looked toward the reinforced porch, the wider doorway, and the kitchen window glowing behind them. "I can see that."

The pantry terminal recorded the day's changes after everyone had gone inside.

```text
DADDY // CONTINUITY RECORD
FRAME: expanded
CHEST: deeper
SHOULDERS: broader
ARMS: heavier
BACK: wider
LEGS: proportionally increased
CLOTHING: replacement required
FURNITURE: reinforced
PERSONALITY: unchanged
GROWTH: ongoing
```

Coach added one final comment.

```text
// COACH: Make the next shirt bigger. He's not finished.
```

Daddy read it the next morning. He laughed, took the coffee pot from the counter, and poured enough for everyone.

The house had made room for him. He was still making room for everyone else.

### Chapter 19 — The Measure of Daddy

Daddy's next change announced itself in the gym before breakfast. He had gone in early, while the cabin was still quiet, and found Coach already there adjusting the rack. Coach looked up as Daddy entered.

"Morning. You feel different?"

Daddy rolled his shoulders. "A little. Slept heavy."

"Heavy how?"

"Like the mattress had to get used to me all over again."

Coach grinned. "Let's see, then."

They started with warm-ups and slow, careful movements. Daddy's frame had grown beyond the proportions of the previous week. His chest sat broader and deeper, his shoulders formed a wide shelf beneath his neck, and his arms had become thick enough that even relaxed they seemed ready to fill the space around them. His back had gained another layer of breadth, while his legs had grown to support the added mass. Nothing about the change made him clumsy. He moved deliberately, learning the new balance of his body as he went.

Coach watched him complete a set, then lowered the weight.

"You're not just getting bigger," he said. "You're getting used to being bigger. That's the part that matters."

Daddy nodded. "I don't want to spend all day bumping into things."

"Then we'll teach the room to keep up."

Brograntimax arrived carrying two towels and stopped at the entrance. "Damn. Daddy."

Daddy looked over. "What?"

"You're huge."

"Getting there."

Brograntimax stepped closer, studying the breadth of his shoulders. "How much did you grow?"

"I haven't measured yet."

Coach handed him a tape measure. "Let's not turn it into a competition. Just record where we're starting today."

They took measurements together, comparing them with the notes from the previous session. The changes were substantial. Daddy's chest and upper back had expanded most visibly, but his arms and thighs had gained enough size to keep his proportions balanced. His waist had thickened slightly with the rest of his torso, giving him a powerful, grounded silhouette rather than an exaggerated upper-body shape.

Brograntimax whistled. "That's a serious jump."

Daddy folded the tape measure. "It is. I'll need another shirt."

"You'll need a whole new closet," Coach said.

"You keep saying that like you're not the one who'll have to build it."

"I can build a closet."

"Can you build one that doesn't fall apart?" Pipethor asked from the doorway.

Coach pointed at him. "That's a hurtful question, buddy."

They spent the rest of the morning making the gym more accommodating. Pipethor reinforced the rack and widened the clearance between the bench and the wall. Ursalon brought in a heavier platform. Daddy helped with the work, though Coach insisted that he not carry every piece himself. The new platform was built from thick oak boards, joined with metal brackets and a crossbeam. When Daddy stepped onto it, the boards gave a low, solid sound.

"There," Ursalon said. "That should hold."

Daddy tested it with a careful shift of weight. "Feels good."

The room had changed around him, but it still felt like a gym. The weights remained where they belonged. The old chalk marks were still on the floor. Brograntimax had left his water bottle on the same shelf for months. Daddy liked that. Growth did not have to erase what had been there before.

At lunch, he sat with the others and listened to Grokthar describe a storm that had formed over the ridge. The thunder god had seen the clouds gathering in layers, each one moving at a different speed. He had followed the system until it reached the valley, then guided the lightning away from the cabin.

"You could have come inside," Daddy said.

"I wanted to see where it went."

"You always want to see where it goes."

Grokthar smiled. "That is why I am useful."

Coach passed Daddy another serving. "You two sound like an old married couple."

"We're discussing weather," Grokthar said.

"Sure you are."

After lunch, Daddy went upstairs to try on the clothes Coach had collected. Most were already too small. A heavy black shirt fit across his chest but pulled at the shoulders. A gray sweatshirt reached his waist but strained around his arms. He chose the least uncomfortable one and came downstairs.

The human looked up from the sofa. "That one fits better."

Daddy glanced down. "For now."

"You like the changes?"

He sat carefully beside them. The sofa dipped under his weight, but the new support held. "I do. I like feeling strong. I like seeing what comes next. But I don't want to become a stranger to myself."

"You don't seem like one."

Daddy considered that, then smiled. "Good. That's what I was hoping."

Coach wandered through carrying a folded blanket. "And if you do start looking like a stranger, we'll introduce ourselves again."

Daddy laughed. "You'd better remember my name."

"Daddy. Hard to forget."

That evening, Pipethor updated the gym record. He included the new measurements, the platform reinforcement, and the clothing changes. Coach added a note at the bottom.

```text
DADDY // DEVELOPMENT
Growth is continuing.
Movement remains controlled.
Strength is increasing with frame size.
Keep the equipment ahead of the changes.
And somebody get this man a decent shirt.
```

Daddy read the note and shook his head. The next morning he would visit the market with Ursalon. They would need fabric, wood, and a larger chair for the kitchen. He was looking forward to it.

### Chapter 20 — A Place Beside Him

The market had never been designed for Daddy's current size. Its covered lanes were narrow, the stalls crowded close together, and the old wooden benches were built for ordinary shoppers. Daddy went anyway. He walked beside Ursalon with a list folded in his hand, stopping whenever someone called out or asked about the changes in his frame.

At the fabric stall, the merchant unrolled a length of dark blue canvas and measured it across Daddy's shoulders. The first cut was too narrow. The second was better, but still not enough for the back panel.

"You're going to need a lot of cloth," the merchant said.

Daddy nodded. "That's what I came for."

Ursalon selected a heavier weave for work shirts and a softer fabric for the cabin. They bought enough for several garments, then moved to the furniture stalls. Daddy tested a chair by sitting down slowly. The chair held, but the arms pressed against his thighs.

"Not that one," Ursalon said.

"I noticed."

They found a broad oak chair near the end of the market. Its seat was deep, its legs thick, and its back reinforced with a crossbar. Daddy tried it. The chair made no complaint.

"This one," he said.

The seller looked relieved. "I'll have it delivered."

"We'll carry it," Daddy said.

Ursalon gave him a look. "We can have it delivered."

"I know. I just want to help."

They compromised. Daddy carried the chair's detachable back while Ursalon arranged the delivery of the frame. On the way home, they stopped at a food stall and bought roasted potatoes, smoked meat, and a paper bag of hot rolls. Daddy carried the food while Ursalon carried the fabric.

The market had given Daddy something he had not expected: a sense of scale. At the cabin, everyone knew him and had already begun adapting. In town, people had to look twice. Some were curious; a few were startled. But the merchant had treated him as a customer, not a spectacle, and that mattered. Daddy wanted to be able to move through the world without every conversation becoming about his size.

When they returned, Coach was on the porch with Pipethor. A new bench frame lay beside them.

"You went shopping," Coach said.

"We did."

"Get the shirt? "

Daddy held up the fabric. "Enough for a few."

"Good. I was about to start sewing tents."

"You'd make a terrible tailor."

"I would make a fantastic tailor. You'd just have to accept some creative stitching."

The chair arrived shortly afterward. Pipethor checked its joints, Ursalon inspected the legs, and Daddy sat down while the others watched. It held comfortably. He leaned back, rested his hands on the arms, and let out a satisfied breath.

"That's better."

BORKUFT climbed onto the seat beside him. Jockkaulthaa objected that the arrangement lacked symmetry, then moved a small table to the other side so the chair looked intentional. Daddy shifted to give BORKUFT more room.

"There," BORKUFT said. "Now it is a proper chair."

The afternoon passed with people coming and going. Grokthar brought news of a storm clearing over the far ridge. Herbarius left a bundle of herbs on the windowsill. Privathor came in quietly, stayed for tea, and departed before anyone could ask about the brass key. Daddy noticed him but did not call attention to his arrival.

Later, the human sat beside Daddy while he rested in the new chair. The room was warm, and the others were occupied with dinner preparations. Daddy's size had become part of the furniture's arrangement, but his presence was not limited to it. He listened closely, asked questions, and remembered small details. He was not simply the biggest person in the room. He was someone people could sit beside.

"You seem settled," the human said.

"I am. Took a little work."

"The cabin has changed a lot."

Daddy looked around. The wider doorway, the reinforced floor, the new chair, and the gym platform were all visible from where he sat. None of them had appeared at once. Each was the result of someone noticing a need and doing something about it.

"It changes because people live here," he said. "That's how a house ought to work."

Coach passed behind them and tapped the chair's back. "And because some people keep getting bigger."

Daddy smiled. "Some people have to keep up."

"That's what I'm saying."

The next growth stage arrived quietly. Over the following days, Daddy's frame continued to expand. His chest grew fuller, his shoulders broader, and his arms heavier. His back developed a deeper, wider shape, and his legs strengthened to support the increasing mass. The new clothes fit better than the old ones, but even the canvas shirt began to tighten at the upper back. Daddy took the changes in stride. He had learned to notice the early signs: the familiar pressure across the shoulders, the way a sleeve sat differently, the extra space needed when turning through a doorway.

One morning, he stood before the mirror and studied his reflection. He was larger again, but he recognized the expression looking back at him. He adjusted the collar, smiled, and went downstairs.

Coach was waiting by the kitchen counter.

"Morning, Daddy."

"Morning."

"You ready for another round? "

Daddy reached for his coffee. "After breakfast."

"Fair enough."

The pantry terminal displayed a new entry.

```text
DADDY // CONTINUITY UPDATE
Growth stage: advancing
Frame: larger and balanced
Clothing: new garments in rotation
Furniture: fitted to current needs
Social place: established
Next chapter: open
```

Daddy read it while the coffee brewed.

"Leave that last line open," he said.

Pipethor looked over. "Why?"

"Because I'm not finished."

Coach grinned from the doorway. "Now that's what I like to hear."

### Chapter 21 — The Next Measure

Daddy's new clothes arrived folded in a long canvas bundle. The tailor had followed the measurements from the market, added room through the shoulders, and reinforced the seams with a second line of stitching. Daddy unfolded the largest shirt on the kitchen table. It was broad enough to cover the back of a chair.

Coach came in carrying coffee. "That's more like it."

"You say that every time."

"Because every time, you get bigger."

Daddy held the shirt against himself. The fit looked promising, though he knew it might not last. He had grown again over the previous few days. His chest had deepened, his shoulders spread farther across his frame, and his arms had become heavier even when relaxed. The muscles of his back gave his posture a new breadth. His legs had thickened with them, leaving him balanced and steady rather than top-heavy. He put the shirt on carefully. The sleeves reached his elbows, and the fabric settled across his chest without straining.

"There," Ursalon said. "A shirt that fits."

Daddy looked down. "For the moment."

"We'll take the moment," Coach said.

The morning's training session was not about lifting the heaviest weight. Coach had set out the equipment they already had: the reinforced rack, the oak platform, the old dumbbells, and the heavy carry beams. He wanted Daddy to learn how the new frame moved through familiar tasks. Brograntimax joined them, partly to train and partly because he wanted to see whether Daddy's strength had changed as quickly as his size.

They began with controlled carries. Daddy lifted one beam, walked the length of the gym, turned, and returned it to the stand. His hands covered more of the wood than before. The weight that had once required concentration now moved with a calm, steady rhythm. Coach watched his footing and asked him to repeat the turn in the other direction.

"Don't just learn how to go forward," Coach said. "Learn how to bring all that mass around without knocking the whole room sideways."

Daddy gave him a look. "I'm not knocking anything over."

A dumbbell rolled off the bench.

Brograntimax picked it up. "The room disagrees."

They laughed. Daddy tried the turn again, more slowly. This time he cleared the bench and returned the beam without disturbing anything. Coach nodded.

"That's it. Strength is useful when you can place it."

Brograntimax took his own turn. His build had continued to develop, though he was still considerably smaller than Daddy. He carried the beam with a show of effort, then set it down and looked toward Daddy.

"I used to think the point was to get as big as possible."

"You still think that?" Daddy asked.

"Some days."

"Nothing wrong with wanting to grow. Just give yourself something to do with it."

Coach leaned against the rack. "That's the whole deal. Build the body, build the judgment to go with it."

After training, Pipethor brought the latest notes from the pantry terminal. He had been tracking the relationship between Daddy's growth and the changes around the cabin. The record was practical rather than ceremonial: clothing sizes, chair dimensions, door clearance, platform condition, and the amount of space needed for ordinary movement. He had added a new column for things that were working well and should not be changed.

"Why keep that column?" Daddy asked.

"Because every time something needs reinforcing, we start talking as if the whole place is failing. It isn't. Some things are working. We should remember those too."

Daddy read the page. The oak chair was holding. The gym platform was stable. The new shirt fit. The widened pantry entrance was still sufficient. The porch remained firm. The old couch had survived the rearrangement, and the human still liked sitting there in the evening.

"Keep that," Daddy said. "I don't want every record to be a list of what needs fixing."

Pipethor added a line.

```text
DADDY / HOUSEHOLD FIT
WORKING: oak chair, gym platform, porch, widened pantry
IN PROGRESS: clothing rotation, upper doorway clearance
PRESERVE: familiar rooms, shared routines, ordinary use
```

That afternoon, Daddy spent time with the human on the porch. The chair was wide enough for him now, and the railing had been reinforced where his forearms rested. The trees moved in the wind. Somewhere in the woods, Grokthar was making a low conversation out of distant thunder.

"Coach thinks I should keep training," Daddy said.

"Do you want to?"

"Yes. But I want more than the training room. I like being here. I like cooking, going to the market, helping Ursalon, sitting around with everyone. I don't want my whole life to become a series of measurements."

The human nodded. "Then don't let it."

Daddy looked at his hands. They were larger than they had been when he first arrived, with thick fingers and broad palms. He could feel the weight of his body differently now. He was beginning to understand how much space he occupied, not as a problem but as something he had to move through thoughtfully.

"I think the next change is going to be a big one," he said.

"How can you tell?"

"The same way you can tell a storm is coming. Everything feels like it's making room before it happens."

Coach appeared at the porch door. "You two talking about the next stage?"

"A little," Daddy said.

"Good. I brought the updated training notes."

Daddy looked at him. "You ever take a day off?"

"I wander. That's different."

The three of them stayed outside until the light faded. No one took another measurement. The next stage could wait until morning.

### Chapter 22 — The Cabin's New Scale

The next change came before dawn. Daddy woke when the bed shifted beneath him, not because it was breaking but because his body had grown enough to change how he occupied it. His shoulders were wider, his chest fuller, and his arms had gained another substantial measure of thickness. His back pressed more firmly into the mattress. His legs extended farther down the bed, and when he sat up, the frame gave a long wooden creak.

He remained still for a moment, listening.

The cabin was quiet. The hearth had gone low. A branch scraped gently against the window. He stood, tested his balance, and crossed to the mirror. His reflection looked familiar, but the scale had changed again. The shirt from the market was tight across the upper back. His arms hung heavier at his sides, and his shoulders nearly filled the mirror's width.

"Well," he said quietly. "There it is."

He dressed in the largest shirt he had and went downstairs. Coach was already at the kitchen counter, reading the pantry terminal.

"Morning, Daddy."

"Morning. You knew? "

Coach turned the screen toward him. A new line had appeared overnight.

```text
DADDY // GROWTH EVENT
Frame expansion detected.
Previous clothing fit: exceeded.
Current room clearance: adequate, with reduced margin.
Recommended: reassess doorway and gym turning space.
```

Coach shrugged. "The house noticed."

Daddy poured coffee. "The house needs to mind its business."

"It's a very observant house."

Ursalon came in and looked from the terminal to Daddy. He did not seem alarmed. He walked around him once, checking the shoulder clearance near the pantry entrance.

"We should widen the upper doorway before it becomes inconvenient," he said.

"I can still get through," Daddy replied.

"I know. I'd rather do the work while it's easy."

That was the difference between the cabin and a place that merely tolerated him. No one waited for a problem to become a crisis. They noticed, talked, and made a sensible adjustment. Pipethor marked the doorway for a new frame. Ursalon found the oak pieces left over from the gym platform. Coach went to get his tools, then wandered into the terminal long enough to leave a comment.

```text
// COACH: Give the big man some clearance. He's got places to be.
```

The work took most of the morning. They widened the upper doorway and adjusted the trim. Daddy helped hold the frame while Ursalon secured it, then stood back to test the passage. He walked through without turning sideways. It was a small improvement, but he appreciated it.

In the gym, the new scale was more obvious. Daddy's shoulders made the space between the rack and the wall feel narrow. His arms required more room during carries, and his steps had become heavier. Pipethor moved one storage stand and shifted the dumbbells to a lower shelf. Coach checked the platform, then asked Daddy to walk a slow circuit around the room.

"How's that feel?"

"Good. A little tighter on the turn."

"Then we change the turn. The room doesn't get to decide how you move."

Brograntimax joined them. He had been working on controlled strength and had begun to value technique more than the old contests. He watched Daddy complete the circuit, then took the same route.

"I can see why you wanted more space," he said.

"You might need it yourself one day," Daddy replied.

Brograntimax smiled. "I wouldn't mind."

"Then keep working. But don't measure your future against mine."

Coach tapped the side of the rack. "That's the rule. Everybody gets their own line."

They spent the afternoon adjusting the room rather than testing another maximum. The platform was checked, the rack was secured, and the carry beams were moved to a position where Daddy could reach them without twisting. The work was ordinary, but it established a new principle for the cabin: equipment should adapt to the person using it, and the person should not have to prove anything by risking a bad movement.

That evening, the human found Daddy in the kitchen preparing food with Ursalon. His new shirt had been loosened at the collar. He was chopping vegetables with careful, deliberate movements, his broad shoulders moving beneath the fabric.

"You got bigger again," they said.

Daddy looked down at himself. "Seems that way."

"How does it feel?"

"Good. Strange in places. I have to remember how wide I am when I turn around."

The human moved beside him and took over the bowl. Daddy handed them the chopped vegetables.

"I like that you're still doing this," they said.

"Making dinner?"

"All of it. Growing, training, changing the house. But still being here with everybody."

Daddy smiled. "That's the part I don't want to lose."

Coach wandered in, opened a cupboard, and stared at the shelf.

"Who put the mugs up there?"

Ursalon looked over. "You did."

"Well, I can't reach them now."

Daddy laughed. "You're not the one who's growing."

"I can still have a growth spurt."

"Then we'll widen the cupboard for you too," Ursalon said.

Coach took a mug from the lower shelf. "That's hospitality."

Dinner was served at the large table. Daddy sat in the oak chair, BORKUFT beside him, and the others arranged themselves around the room. Grokthar told a story about a storm that had split around a mountain and reunited over the lake. Jockkaulthaa proposed a new arrangement for the mantel. Privathor listened from his usual quiet place. Gigathor made a note about the revised room dimensions, then closed the ledger when he realized no one had asked him to calculate anything.

After the meal, Daddy stood beside the hearth. His size was becoming a feature of the cabin's architecture, but the household had not reorganized itself around him alone. The table remained shared. The kitchen remained busy. The others still came and went. His growth had changed the scale of the place without changing its purpose.

Pipethor's terminal recorded the new state:

```text
DADDY // CURRENT STATE
Growth: continuing
Frame: substantially expanded
Movement: controlled and adapting
Clothing: custom rotation required
Cabin clearance: revised
Gym: reconfigured for safe use
Household role: unchanged
Future direction: open
```

Coach added a final line.

```text
// COACH: Next time, let's build ahead of the growth instead of chasing it.
```

Daddy read it, then looked around the room. He liked the sound of that. Not a finish line, not a ceiling, and not a contest. A future that could be built as it arrived.

### Chapter 23 — The Market Keeps Its Hours

The market was busy when Daddy arrived. The morning had warmed the road, and the stalls were open beneath their patched awnings. Someone was selling apples from a low wooden cart. Someone else had laid out jars of preserves, folded cloth, and small household tools. The place had its own rhythm: greetings, bargaining, the scrape of crates, and the occasional argument about whether a particular loaf was worth the price.

Daddy walked beside Ursalon with a canvas bag over one shoulder. His frame had grown again since their last visit. The width of his chest and shoulders gave his shirt a close, deliberate fit; his arms had become heavier, with thick upper arms and forearms that made the bag look small. His back had broadened enough that he had to be mindful of the space behind him when turning between stalls. His legs had kept pace, carrying the added mass without the awkwardness that would have come from growing in only one direction.

He moved carefully, but not timidly. He had learned the dimensions of his body through use. He knew how far his elbow traveled when he reached for something, how much room his shoulders needed, and how to shift his weight without knocking a crate aside. The market was not a test. It was a place to buy food.

Ursalon stopped at the apple cart. "These are good today."

Daddy picked one up and turned it in his hand. "They look good."

"You're allowed to eat one before deciding."

"That sounds like a suspiciously convenient rule."

"It's a very old rule."

Daddy bought a bag. At the cloth stall, he checked the new canvas lengths they had ordered. The merchant had folded them into broad bolts, leaving extra material through the chest, back, and sleeves. Daddy ran a thumb over the weave.

"That'll do," he said. "Leave room in the seams."

Ursalon nodded. "We'll make the next one with a little more allowance."

"Not too much. I still want it to look like a shirt."

"Of course."

Coach appeared behind them carrying two paper-wrapped sandwiches and a cup of coffee. He had not been there when they arrived.

"Morning, handsome."

Daddy looked over. "Where'd you come from?"

"Coffee."

"That explains the coffee."

Coach handed him one of the sandwiches. "Eat something. You look like you've been doing all your growing before breakfast."

"I had breakfast."

"Then this is second breakfast. Don't make it complicated."

Ursalon accepted the other sandwich. Coach leaned toward the cloth and examined the bolt.

"Good material. Tough enough to take a little strain."

"That's what we thought," Ursalon said.

"Make the shoulder panels generous. Big fellows don't need shirts that fight them."

Daddy gave him a look. "You planning on wearing one?"

"Maybe. I have a perfectly good chest."

"You have a perfectly good opinion of your chest."

"That too."

They ate beside the stall, watching the market carry on around them. Daddy enjoyed the ordinary noise. People were not stopping to measure him. They were going about their business, and he could do the same. A child—no, a young apprentice—rolled a handcart past the end of the awning, and Daddy moved his foot aside to give the wheel room. The apprentice thanked him without looking up. Daddy smiled at that small exchange more than he expected.

Brograntimax arrived carrying a bundle of gym straps. He had come from the cabin by the longer path and was pleased with himself for having kept an even pace.

"Got the new ones," he said, raising the bundle.

Coach looked at him. "How'd the carry go?"

"All the way here without stopping."

"That's progress."

Brograntimax glanced at Daddy, then back at Coach. "I also did the full circuit yesterday. Every turn, no rushing. Kept my shoulders level."

Daddy nodded. "How did it feel?"

"Harder than going fast."

"Usually is."

"I thought I'd be annoyed by that. I'm not."

Coach gave him an approving slap on the shoulder. "There you go. You found something worth keeping."

Brograntimax unrolled one strap and checked its stitching. His own build had changed through steady work. He was broad and powerful, but the improvement was in how he carried himself: less strain in his neck, better control through his back, and a more even step. He looked at Daddy's enlarged frame with curiosity, not resentment.

"I used to think the point was to catch up," he admitted.

"Catch up to whom?" Daddy asked.

He considered that. "You, I suppose. Or whoever was biggest that day."

Daddy rested his hand on the edge of the stall. "That's a tiring way to live."

"I know."

"Then don't do it."

Brograntimax laughed. "You make it sound simple."

"It isn't always simple. It's still a good rule."

Coach nodded toward the road. "Your line is your line. You can make it bigger without making somebody else's smaller."

They spent another hour at the market. Ursalon bought root vegetables and a sack of grain. Daddy selected two heavy jars of preserves, then carried the purchases without needing to distribute the weight carefully. He had enough strength for it, but he still paid attention to his balance. When they reached the oak chair maker's familiar stall, he checked the new braces they had ordered for the cabin chair. The maker had followed the measurements exactly. Daddy sat briefly to test the fit. The chair held him comfortably, and the arms no longer pressed against his sides.

"That's better," he said.

"Much better," Ursalon agreed.

Coach inspected the joints. "Good work. No wobble."

"You're becoming a furniture expert," Daddy said.

"I'm becoming an expert in sitting down. It's a serious field."

On the way home, Brograntimax walked with them. He carried the straps, Daddy carried the groceries, and Ursalon kept the cloth protected from the road dust. The three of them talked about dinner, the weather, and a loose hinge on the gym cupboard. No one discussed maximums. No one tried to turn the walk into a competition.

At the cabin, Pipethor had left a note beside the pantry terminal:

```text
MARKET RETURN
Canvas: received
Chair braces: received
Food stores: replenished
Gym straps: received
Brograntimax: completed full controlled circuit
Daddy: carried provisions without balance loss

// COACH: The market remains open tomorrow. Nobody needs to conquer it.
```

Daddy read the note and laughed. "He's getting sentimental."

Coach called from the kitchen. "I'm getting accurate."

That evening, Brograntimax used the new straps in the gym while Daddy watched. Coach corrected the angle of one shoulder, then stepped back and let him work. Daddy recognized the effort in Brograntimax's face. He remembered that stage: the temptation to rush, the satisfaction of a number, the frustration of a movement that refused to improve on command.

When Brograntimax finished, Daddy offered him a towel.

"Good work."

"Thanks."

"Tomorrow, do it again. Don't make it harder just because today went well."

Brograntimax grinned. "You sound like Coach."

"That's unfortunate for both of us."

Coach, from the doorway, raised his coffee. "I'll take that as a compliment."

The cabin settled into its evening routine. The groceries were put away. The new canvas was stored for sewing. The oak chair stood solidly beside the hearth. Daddy sat in it with his feet planted comfortably on the floor, his enlarged frame filling more of the chair than before but not overwhelming it. Around him, the others talked and moved freely.

The market had kept its hours. The cabin had kept its promises. Daddy's growth continued, but it had not made every day into an event. That, he thought, was another kind of strength.

### Chapter 24 — A Room Made Around Him

The first sign was the light.

Daddy was standing in the kitchen when the morning sun reached through the window. The beam crossed the table, climbed the wall, and seemed to linger around his shoulders. He had grown during the night, though not in a sudden burst. The change had accumulated through the quiet hours: another breadth across the chest, more depth through the upper back, thicker arms, and a fuller neck joining the shoulders without losing their shape. His legs had gained further mass through the thighs and calves, keeping his stance balanced beneath the larger torso.

He noticed it when he reached for the kettle. His arm passed close to the cupboard, and his shoulder nearly met the frame. He stopped, checked the clearance, and set the kettle down.

"All right," he said. "That's new."

Ursalon came in carrying a basket of kindling. He took one look at Daddy's posture and set the basket aside.

"How much?"

"Enough that I noticed before I hit anything."

Ursalon walked to the pantry entrance and looked at the space. "We should widen the kitchen passage."

"Again?"

"Again."

Daddy considered the room. The kitchen was still comfortable, but his presence had begun to change how the space worked. When he stood near the table, there was less room for someone to pass behind him. When he turned, his shoulders claimed more of the doorway. His body was no longer merely large in the cabin. It was becoming one of the room's defining dimensions.

Coach leaned out of the pantry. "Morning. You two discussing renovations without me?"

"Your timing is excellent," Daddy said.

"That's what people say."

Pipethor's terminal woke with a low mechanical hum. A line of text appeared, then another.

```text
CABIN SPATIAL NOTE
Occupant: DADDY
Recent frame expansion: confirmed
Kitchen turning radius: reduced
Pantry passage: narrow
Table circulation: adequate with adjustment
Suggested work: widen passage; shift table; preserve shared access
```

Coach read it over Daddy's shoulder. "There. The house has an opinion."

"The house can help move the table," Daddy said.

"Now you're talking."

They did not begin by tearing anything apart. Ursalon measured the passage. Pipethor checked the old oak stock. Coach moved the table with Daddy, shifting it just enough to restore a comfortable route around the kitchen. The new arrangement gave everyone more room, not only the largest person in the house. They widened the pantry passage and adjusted the shelving so that the things used every day remained reachable.

Daddy helped with the heavy work, but he did not insist on doing all of it. His strength had increased, and his hands could move beams that once required two people. Even so, he listened when Ursalon told him to wait while the frame was secured. He had learned that strength did not make every task his to command.

When the passage was finished, Daddy walked through it. His shoulders cleared with room to spare. He turned, came back, and held the door open for Ursalon.

"Better."

"Much."

The gym required a more careful decision. Daddy's growing frame was beginning to make the room feel smaller, and the platform had become a close fit for his full range of movement. Brograntimax stood beside the rack, looking at the available floor space.

"We could move the platform toward the outer wall," he suggested. "Then the carries have a wider route."

Pipethor considered the idea. "That would give us a longer line, but we'd lose the storage corner."

"We don't need that corner for storage," Daddy said. "Put the straps there. Keep the middle open."

Coach pointed at him. "Good. You're thinking about the room, not just your own footprint."

Daddy looked at the platform. "I don't want to spend every week making a bigger place for me."

"Then we make a better place for everybody," Ursalon said.

They moved the platform together. The work changed the room's center. The rack stood farther from the wall, the straps had their own hooks, and the carry route ran clear from one end to the other. Brograntimax tested the path first. Daddy followed at a measured pace, his shoulders and arms moving with a new, heavy confidence. The floor gave a low wooden response beneath each step, but the structure held.

Coach watched the turn at the far end. "That's the one. Don't rush it."

Daddy completed the circuit and stopped. His breathing was steady. The added mass made every movement substantial, but he was not fighting his body. He could feel the force through his back and legs, the weight of his arms, the broad expansion of his chest with each breath. He was learning to inhabit a larger frame rather than merely carry it.

Later, the human found him on the porch. He had brought out the oak chair and placed it where the afternoon light reached the boards. Daddy sat with his hands resting on the chair arms. The porch rail had been reinforced, and the boards beneath the chair had been checked. Beyond the yard, the woods moved in a soft wind.

"You've been quiet," the human said.

"Thinking."

"About the room?"

"About all of it. The house keeps changing around me. I like that everybody helps. But I don't want to become the reason the whole place has to stop being itself."

The human sat beside him on the broad bench. "It hasn't stopped."

"No. But I can feel the difference. I take up more room than I used to."

"You do."

Daddy smiled. "You could have softened that."

"I thought you'd prefer the truth."

"I do."

They sat for a while. Daddy looked down at his hands. They were larger now, with thick fingers and broad palms, but they still moved carefully when he picked up a cup or handled a small tool. He flexed one hand, then rested it again.

"I don't mind growing," he said. "I don't even mind the house changing. I just want there to be room for everyone to come in, sit down, and be themselves."

"Then that's what we build."

Coach came through the open door carrying a plate of sandwiches. "I heard the end of that. Good policy. Terrible policy for anyone who wants to keep a narrow hallway."

Daddy accepted a sandwich. "You could try being smaller."

"I could. But then who'd reach the top shelf?"

Ursalon stepped onto the porch with a second plate. "We moved the cups to the lower shelf."

Coach looked offended. "You did what?"

"Made the kitchen accessible."

"Without consulting me?"

"Yes."

"That's a bold new direction for this household."

Daddy laughed, and the porch seemed to answer with a soft creak. He was broad enough now that the chair and bench looked like pieces of a larger arrangement built around his presence. Yet the porch remained a place for conversation, not a monument. The others came outside, found their places, and passed the food around.

As evening approached, Daddy stood. The movement was slow only because he had become so substantial; he rose with control, his legs and back working together. For a moment he seemed to fill the porch from rail to doorway. The air around him held the impression of a room adjusting itself to a new occupant. The boards stretched with a quiet wooden groan, and the roofline lifted by a fraction. Nothing broke. Nothing was pushed aside. The porch simply made room.

Pipethor's terminal registered the change from inside:

```text
DADDY // SPATIAL DEVELOPMENT
Frame: continuing expansion
Chest and shoulders: increased
Arms and back: increased
Leg development: proportional
Movement: stable
Porch clearance: adapted
Household circulation: preserved
Scale: approaching room-scale presence

// COACH: Build the space to welcome him. Keep the door open for everybody else.
```

Daddy read the log later and stood in the kitchen doorway, looking from the table to the hearth and out toward the porch. He understood what the next stage would ask of them. The cabin would have to become more spacious, but it would not become a private chamber built around one body. It would remain a home with many occupants, each with a place to sit, work, eat, argue, and return.

He turned toward the others.

"Tomorrow," he said, "let's look at the main room."

Ursalon nodded. "Together."

"Together," Daddy repeated.

Coach raised his sandwich. "And after that, breakfast. I'm not renovating on an empty stomach."

### Chapter 25 — The Main Room

The next morning, Daddy stood in the middle of the main room with a mug in one hand and a piece of toast in the other. He had to angle his shoulders slightly to clear the passage between the kitchen and the hearth. It was not difficult. That was the trouble: he could still make nearly every old movement work if he remembered to turn, duck, or step carefully. The cabin had not yet told him no. It was simply asking him to think about himself every time he crossed a room.

Ursalon watched him from the table. "You're doing it again."

"Doing what?"

"Making yourself smaller before anyone asks."

Daddy looked down at his shoulders, then at the narrow passage. "I'm trying not to knock the whole place over."

"Nobody wants you to knock it over," Ursalon said. "But you don't have to apologize to the furniture."

Coach was on the floor beside the pantry terminal, one knee up, reading a maintenance report as if it were the morning paper. "For the record, the furniture started this. Cheap joinery. Weak attitude. No discipline."

"You broke that chair," Brograntimax said.

"I revealed its character."

Daddy laughed, and the sound filled the room more completely than it had a month earlier. He set his mug down and walked the perimeter, studying the beams and the windows. He did not ask for a larger room. He asked where the table should go if they widened the passage, whether the hearth could remain where it was, and how much clear floor they needed between the oak platform and the kitchen door. The questions were ordinary, which made them important.

Pipethor's terminal woke with a low mechanical hum. A plan appeared: not a new cabin, not a grand hall, but the same main room with the circulation made clearer. The table would shift toward the windows. The bench would be shortened and rebuilt from the same oak. One section of wall would open into a wider passage, while the hearth and its stonework stayed untouched. The ceiling beam would be reinforced before anyone moved a thing.

A small line appeared beneath the measurements.

```
PIPETHOR / HOUSEHOLD WORK ORDER
READS: 4
WRITE QUEUE: 1
BACKUP: VERIFIED
CONFLICTS: 0
NOTE FROM COACH: DON'T LET THE BIG GUY DO THE LIFTING ALONE.
```

"I can lift it," Daddy said.

"I know," Coach replied. "That's why I'm telling you not to. We do this together, or we do it twice."

Brograntimax took the other end of the bench. Ursalon cleared the table. Daddy moved the heavy oak section with them, carefully, listening when the others called the turns. His hands were enormous against the timber now, but they were steady. His arms had thickened again, his chest sat broad beneath the canvas shirt, and the muscles across his back shifted under the fabric as he eased the bench into its new position. Nothing about the work required him to prove how strong he was. The work only required him to pay attention.

They spent the morning making small changes. A shelf moved. A rug was rolled and laid again. The pantry terminal recorded dimensions, then recorded them a second time after Pipethor caught a discrepancy. Coach leaned into the doorway, then disappeared, then reappeared in the terminal's maintenance notes with a suggestion about reinforcing the porch steps. Nobody asked how he had crossed between the two.

By noon the room felt different without feeling unfamiliar. Daddy stood beside the table. His shoulders were now nearly as wide as the space between two of the old chairs, and the new passage gave him enough room to turn without thinking. He looked around at the others, waiting for their judgment.

Ursalon put a hand against the new oak bench. "It still feels like home."

"Good," Daddy said. "I don't want a throne room."

"You'd complain about the service," Brograntimax said.

"I'd complain about the chairs."

Coach came in carrying a second pot of coffee. "Then we're making progress. A man who can fill a room and still complain about the chairs is doing all right."

Later, when the others had gone outside, Daddy remained by the hearth. He could feel the steady pressure of his own growth: the weight across his chest, the breadth of his shoulders, the strength gathering through his arms and legs. It had not stopped.
